At Sunday Matins (§19), the Resurrection Dismissal Troparion is chanted once and the Festal Troparion twice; the Resurrection Canon is combined with the Canon of the Cross, culminating in the solemn chanting of the Ninth Ode of the Feast with both of its Katavasiai (*Μυστικὸς εἶ, Θεοτόκε* and *Ὁ διὰ βρώσεως τοῦ ξύλου*). At the Divine Liturgy (§20), the proper Festal Antiphons are retained: at Antiphon 2, the refrain is *Ὁ σαρκὶ σταυρωθείς*; at the Little Entrance, the festal Isodikon combines the festal verse with the Sunday refrain *Ὁ ἀναστὰς ἐκ νεκρῶν*; and in place of *Axion Estin*, the festal Zadostoinyk is chanted: *Ὁ διὰ βρώσεως τοῦ ξύλου* (the Heirmos of Ode 9 of the Second Canon of the Cross by St. John of Damascus). The concluding rubrical note defines the structural boundary of the Menologion: beginning on the first Sunday after the Apodosis of the Cross, the appointed Katavasiai transition to the Marian canon *Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου* (“I shall open my mouth”, Tone 4) through November 21 (the Feast of the Entry of the Most Holy Theotokos into the Temple), while the seasonal Kontakion becomes *Προστασία τῶν Χριστιανῶν ἀκαταίσχυντε* (“O Protection of Christians that cannot be put to shame”, Tone 2) through November 8 (the Synaxis of the Archangel Michael and the Bodiless Hosts).

[^157]: The Conception of the Honorable Forerunner, Text-Critical Rectification of Stichira Count, and the Theotokos of Kythera with St. Thecla (Violakis, pp. 184–185, September 23, §§1–3, and September 24, §1). September 23 marks the Feast of the Conception of the Honorable and Glorious Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John (*Ἡ Σύλληψις τοῦ τιμίου Προδρόμου*, §§1–3), which celebrates the supernatural intervention of God in the conception of John by the elderly and barren Elizabeth following the angelic annunciation to the Priest Zacharias in the Holy of Holies (Luke 1:5–25). When this feast concurs with Sunday (§§2–3), Great Vespers appoints ten Stichira at “Lord, I have cried”. A significant text-critical divergence exists between the editio princeps of the Greek text and its original English facing page: the Greek text definitively specifies six Resurrection Stichira and four of the Forerunner (`Ἀναστάσιμα ς΄ καὶ τοῦ Προδρόμου δ΄`), completing the requisite decuple; the English translation facing leaf in the 1888 edition erroneously recorded “3 Stichira of the Forerunner”, which would have left the decuple incomplete with only nine Stichira. This critical edition rectifies the erratum in conformity with the primary Greek witness and the authoritative Constantinopolitan Menaion. At Matins (§3), the Kathisma *Θυμιῶντι ἐν Ναῷ* (“As Zacharias was offering incense in the temple”) and the Theotokion *Κατεπλάγη Ἰωσήφ* (“Joseph was struck with wonder”) follow Ode 3; at the Divine Liturgy, the Epistle is assigned to the Forerunner (Galatians 4:22–31, allegorizing the barren Sarah and the child of promise Isaac), while the Gospel is that of the Sunday. Section 1 of September 24 details the concurrence on Sunday of the Wonderworking Icon of the Most Holy Theotokos of Kythera (*Παναγία ἡ Μυρτιδιώτισσα*, “Our Lady of the Myrtle Tree”, commemorating the miraculous discovery of the holy icon amidst myrtle branches on the Aegean island of Kythera in the 14th century and the healing of a paralytic) with the Holy Protomartyr and Equal-of-the-Apostles Thecla of Iconium (first century), spiritual daughter and disciple of the Holy Apostle Paul.


## Cohort 21 Footnotes

[^158]: The Apolytikia of Saint Thecla, Patriarchal Compositions (Cyril VI and Constantius I), and Folio Continuity (Violakis, pp. 186–187, September 24, §§2–3 concl., and Troparia). September 24 concludes the combined commemoration of the Wonderworking Icon of the Most Holy Theotokos Myrtidiotissa of Kythera (*Παναγία ἡ Μυρτιδιώτισσα*) and the Holy Protomartyr and Equal-to-the-Apostles Thecla of Iconium. The liturgical rubrics at §2 (Sunday Matins) appoint the Dismissal Troparion of the Theotokos (*Τῆς Μυρτιδιωτίσσης*, Tone 4) followed by that of Saint Thecla. At §3, the Divine Liturgy appoints the Sunday Epistle and the Gospel for the Theotokos (Luke 10:38–42; 11:27–28). When the feast falls on a weekday, the entire service of the Menaion is sung. Following the rubrics, the Typikon incorporates two historically significant proper Dismissal Troparia for Saint Thecla composed or retrieved by two 19th-century Ecumenical Patriarchs of Constantinople: First, the Dismissal Troparion in Tone 3, *Θείου κήρυκος διδασκαλίας* ("Inflamed with desire for Thy Creator through the teaching of the divine preacher"), composed in 1813 by Patriarch Cyril VI (reigned 1813–1818; martyred in Adrianople at the outbreak of the Greek War of Independence in 1821; glorified as a hieromartyr). Second, an alternative Dismissal Troparion in Tone 2, *Ὡς τῷ Παύλῳ μαθητευθεῖσα* ("Having been taught by Paul, O all-praised one"), transcribed in 1832 by Patriarch Constantius I of Sinai (reigned 1830–1834; renowned scholar, hymnographer, and former Archbishop of Sinai) from an ancient parchment manuscript (*εὑρεθὲν ἐν μεμβράνῃ*). In the printed 1888 edition, the introductory rubric for Constantius I's troparion appears at the foot of printed page 186 (leaf p201 / English facing leaf p202), while the troparion text itself continues across the leaf boundary onto the top of printed page 188 (leaf p203 / English facing leaf p204).

[^159]: Feast of the Metastasis of Saint John the Theologian, Ceremonial Standing for Paremiae, and Folio Transition (Violakis, pp. 188–189, September 26, §§1–3 incipit). September 26 marks the solemn commemoration of the Falling Asleep (Metastasis) of the Holy, Glorious Apostle and Evangelist John the Theologian (*Ἡ μετάστασις τοῦ ἁγίου ἐνδόξου Ἀποστόλου καὶ Εὐαγγελιστοῦ Ἰωάννου τοῦ Θεολόγου*). At Great Vespers (§1), following the First Stasis of the First Kathisma of the Psalter (Psalms 1–3 LXX), three Prosomia are sung at "Lord, I have cried" doubled unto 6, followed by the Entrance with the censer (*Φῶς ἱλαρόν*) and the daily Prokeimenon. The three festal Old Testament readings (Paremiae) are unique in the Byzantine liturgical corpus: rather than being drawn from the Old Testament prophets or wisdom literature, they are excerpted from the New Testament Catholic Epistles of Saint John himself (1 John 3:21–4:6; 1 John 4:11–16; and 1 John 4:20–5:5). Because these readings derive from apostolic canonical Scripture, the Great Church of Constantinople prescribes an exceptional ceremonial rubric: *(καθ’ ἅ ἡμεῖς ἱστάμεθα, ὁ δὲ Διάκονος ἐκφωνεῖ Πρόσχωμεν, Σοφία, Πρόσχωμεν)* ("during which we stand upright, as the Deacon exclaims: Let us be attentive! Wisdom! Let us be attentive!"). This elevates the Paremiae to a liturgical honor normally reserved exclusively for the Holy Gospel and apostolic lections. At Matins (§2), the order includes the Liti, the Polyeleos, the First Antiphon of the Anavathmoi of Tone 4 (*Ἐκ νεότητός μου*), the festal Prokeimenon (Psalm 18:5 LXX: *Εἰς πᾶσαν τὴν γῆν*), and the Eleventh Morning Gospel (John 21:15–25, detailing Christ's dialogue with Peter regarding the beloved disciple). Section 3 begins the Divine Liturgy propers at the foot of printed page 188 (leaf p203 / English facing leaf p204: *Τροπάρια ἐκ...*), which breaks syntactically mid-sentence and continues directly onto printed page 190 (leaf p205 / English facing leaf p206: *...τῆς γ΄ καὶ ς΄ Ὠδῆς τοῦ Κανόνος τοῦ Εὐαγγελιστοῦ*).

[^160]: Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II) and Syntactical Leaf Continuation (Violakis, pp. 192–193, October 11–17, §§1–3 incipit). The service of the Holy God-bearing Fathers of the Seventh Ecumenical Council (held in Nicaea in A.D. 787 under Empress Irene and Patriarch Tarasius to anathematize iconoclasm and define the Orthodox veneration of holy icons) is celebrated annually on the Sunday falling between October 11 and 17 inclusive. At Saturday Great Vespers (§1), 6 Resurrection Stichira are joined to 4 Stichira of the Holy Fathers; the Old Testament Paremiae comprise three classic conciliar readings: Genesis 14:14–20 (Melchizedek blessing Abraham), Deuteronomy 1:8–11, 15–17 (Moses establishing wise and righteous judges over the tribes), and Deuteronomy 10:14–21 (Moses recounting the majesty and commandments of the Lord). At Sunday Matins (§2), following the Resurrection Canons, the Canon of the Fathers is sung, culminating in the Katavasiai *Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου*. At the Divine Liturgy (§3), the Beatitudes combine 4 of the Tone with 4 from Ode 6 of the Canon of the Fathers; the appointed Epistle reading is Titus 3:8–15 (*Τέκνον Τίτε, πιστὸς ὁ λόγος*), exhorting the clergy to maintain good works and avoid foolish questions, genealogies, and strivings about the law; and the Gospel reading is Luke 8:5–15 (the Parable of the Sower, appointed for the Fourth Sunday of Luke). The rubric breaks syntactically at the bottom of printed page 193 (leaf p208 / English facing leaf p208: *At...*) across the leaf boundary onto printed page 194 (leaf p209 / English facing leaf p210: *...the Megalynarion: It is truly meet*).

[^161]: The Tripartite Annual Commemoration of the Ecumenical Councils in the Constantinopolitan Typikon (Violakis, pp. 194–195, October 11–17, Concluding Rubrical Note). Appended to the rubric of the Seventh Ecumenical Council on printed page 194 is a fundamental didascalic note (*Σημείωσις*) summarizing the three annual corporate celebrations dedicated to the Holy Fathers of the Ecumenical Councils in the Byzantine liturgical year: (1) The First Ecumenical Council (Nicaea I, A.D. 325, which formulated the initial clauses of the Nicene Creed and anathematized Arius), commemorated on the Seventh Sunday of Pascha (the Sunday after the Ascension of our Lord), with the appointed Gospel pericope from Saint John (John 17:1–13, the High Priestly Prayer of Christ for the unity of the Church); (2) The Fourth Ecumenical Council (Chalcedon, A.D. 451, which confessed the two unconfused and undivided natures of Christ, celebrated jointly with the First Six Ecumenical Councils), commemorated on the Sunday falling between July 13 and 19 inclusive, with the appointed Gospel pericope from Saint Matthew (Matthew 5:14–19, "Ye are the light of the world... Whosoever shall do and teach them, the same shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven"); and (3) The Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, A.D. 787, which restored the veneration of the holy icons), commemorated on the Sunday falling between October 11 and 17 inclusive, with the appointed Gospel pericope from Saint Luke (Luke 8:5–15, the Parable of the Sower, from the Fourth Sunday of Luke). This note provides liturgical coordinators and cantors with an authoritative comparative key to the three major patristic celebrations that anchor the movable and fixed cycles of the Constantinopolitan typikon.

[^162]: Feast of the Holy Apostle and Evangelist Luke, Sunday Concurrence, and Inter-Cohort Transition to Cohort 22 (Violakis, pp. 194–195, October 18, §§1–3 incipit). October 18 commemorates the Holy Apostle and Evangelist Luke, companion of Saint Paul and author of the Third Gospel and the Acts of the Apostles. When the feast occurs on a weekday (§1), the order of the Menaion is sung plain. When the feast concurs with Sunday (§§2–3), Great Vespers on Saturday evening appoints 6 Resurrection Stichira and 4 Stichira of the Evangelist at "Lord, I have cried", followed by the Entrance and the daily Prokeimenon; at the Aposticha, the Doxastikon is assigned to the Evangelist (*Πάνσοφε ἁλιεῦ*) and the Dismissal Troparion is *Ἀπόστολε ἅγιε καὶ εὐαγγελιστὰ Λουκᾶ*. At Sunday Matins (§3), the order combines the Resurrection Kathismata and the 17th Kathisma (Psalm 118 LXX, the Amomos), followed by the Canons (Resurrection and Saint Luke) and the Katavasiai *Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου*. Section 3 concludes at the bottom of printed page 195 (leaf p210 / English facing leaf p210) with the beginning of the propers for the Divine Liturgy: *At the Divine Liturgy: the Typika, and 4 Beatitudes of the Tone and 4 Troparia from the Sixth Ode of the Canon of the Apostle...* This sentence terminates at the physical leaf margin, transitioning directly across the cohort boundary onto printed page 196 (leaf p211), which begins Cohort 22 with the Little Entrance, the dismissal troparia, the Kontakion, and the Epistle (Colossians 4:5–11, 14–18) and Gospel (Luke 10:16–21) readings of the Apostle.


## Cohort 22 Footnotes

[^163]: Feast of the Holy Apostle and Evangelist Luke, Liturgical Propers, and Inter-Cohort Transition (Violakis, pp. 196–197, October 18, § 3 concl.). Physical leaf p211 (printed page 196) and facing English leaf p212 (printed page 197) begin Cohort 22 with the concluding propers of the Divine Liturgy for the feast of Saint Luke the Evangelist when celebrated on a Sunday, continuing directly across the cohort boundary from printed page 195 (leaf p210, Cohort 21). Following the Little Entrance, the dismissal troparia, and the Kontakion *Protection of Christians*, the appointed lections are: Epistle: Colossians 4:5–11, 14–18 (pericope 260, honoring Luke "the beloved physician"); Gospel: Luke 10:16–21 (pericope 51, the mission and return of the Seventy Disciples); at the Megalynarion: *It is truly meet* (*Ἄξιόν ἐστιν*); and the festal Communion Verse: Psalm 18:5 LXX (*Εἰς πᾶσαν τὴν γῆν ἐξῆλθεν ὁ φθόγγος αὐτῶν* / "Their sound hath gone forth into all the earth, and their words unto the ends of the world"). The service concludes with *We have seen the true light* and the customary dismissal.

[^164]: Commemoration of St. Artemios, St. Matrona of Chios, and St. Gerasimos of Cephalonia, Special Festal Booklets, and Sunday Concurrence (Violakis, pp. 196–199, October 20, §§ 1–4). October 20 unites the general Byzantine commemoration of the Holy Great-Martyr Artemios (beheaded under Julian the Apostate in A.D. 363) with two highly venerated post-Byzantine monastic saints whose offices developed in regional pamphlets (*ἰδιαίτεραι φυλλάδες*): the Righteous Mother Matrona of Chios (d. 1462, whose office was composed and popularized across the Aegean islands) and our Righteous Father Gerasimos the New Ascetic of Cephalonia (d. 1579, whose incorrupt relics in Omala form the monastic heart of the Ionian Islands). When October 20 falls on a weekday (§ 1), the first three Stichira at "Lord, I have cried" are sung for Saint Artemios from the general Menaion, followed by three Stichira for Saint Matrona or Saint Gerasimos from their respective booklets, with the Doxastikon assigned to whichever local patron is celebrated. When occurring on a Sunday (§§ 2–4), Saturday Great Vespers prescribes 4 Resurrection Stichira, 3 of Saint Artemios, and 3 of Saint Matrona or Saint Gerasimos; at Matins, the Liti is taken from the booklet of the righteous saint, followed by the Triadic hymns (*Ἄξιόν ἐστιν*) and the third Kathisma reading assigned to the saint; at the Divine Liturgy, the Beatitudes combine 4 of the Tone and 4 from Ode 6 of the Canon of the righteous saint, with the Epistle taken from the saint's office (Galatians 3:23–4:5 for a female ascetic or Galatians 5:22–6:2 for a venerable monk) and the Sunday Gospel.

[^165]: Feast of the Holy Apostle James, Brother of the Lord (Adelphotheos), Sunday Concurrence, and Apolytikion (Violakis, pp. 198–201, October 23, §§ 1–4). October 23 commemorates Saint James the Brother of the Lord (*Ἰάκωβος ὁ Ἀδελφόθεος*), first Bishop of Jerusalem, author of the Catholic Epistle of James, and martyr under the High Priest Ananus II in A.D. 62. On a weekday (§ 1), the office is sung plain from the Menaion. When the feast occurs on a Sunday (§§ 2–4), Great Vespers on Saturday evening appoints 6 Resurrection Stichira and 4 Stichira of the Apostle at "Lord, I have cried"; the Doxastikon is *Αἵματι τοῦ μαρτυρίου* ("With the blood of martyrdom"); at the Aposticha, the Doxastikon is *Τοῦ Ἀρχιποίμενος Χριστοῦ* ("Of Christ the Chief Shepherd"); and the Dismissal Troparion is the ancient troparion of Saint James: *Ὡς τοῦ Κυρίου μαθητὴς ἀνεδέξω, δίκαιε, τὸ Εὐαγγέλιον· ὡς μάρτυς ἔχεις τὸ ἀπαράτρεπτον· τὴν παρρησίαν ὡς ἀδελφόθεος· τὸ πρεσβεύειν ὡς ἱεράρχης* ("As a disciple of the Lord thou didst receive the Gospel, O righteous one; as a martyr thou hast that which cannot be changed; boldness as the Brother of God; and intercession as a hierarch"). At Matins (§ 3), after the Amomos (Psalm 118 LXX) and the Canons, the Katavasiai *Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου* are sung; at the Divine Liturgy (§ 4), the Beatitudes combine 4 of the Tone with 4 from Ode 6 of the Canon of the Apostle, the Epistle is Galatians 1:11–19 (Saint Paul recounting his apostolic calling and his meeting in Jerusalem with "James the Lord's brother"), and the Sunday Gospel is appointed.

[^166]: Feast of the Holy Great-Martyr Demetrios the Myrrh-Streamer and the Commemoration of the Great Earthquake at Constantinople (Violakis, pp. 200–205, October 26, §§ 1–8). October 26 is one of the most solemn feast days of the Byzantine calendar, combining the major martyrdom of Saint Demetrios of Thessalonica (martyred ca. A.D. 306 under Maximian Galerius) with the civil and liturgical commemoration of the cataclysmic earthquake that struck Constantinople on October 26, A.D. 740, under Emperor Leo III the Isaurian (the Violakis heading parenthetically cites 791, reflecting a recensional chronological discrepancy found in certain post-Byzantine liturgical printed synaxaria). At Vespers (§ 1), 6 festal Stichira of Saint Demetrios are sung at "Lord, I have cried", followed by three Old Testament lections (Prophecies: Isaiah 63:15–64:5, 8–9; Jeremiah 2:2–12; and Wisdom of Solomon 3:1–9); at the Aposticha, the penitential Idiomela of the Earthquake are sung, followed by the Doxastikon of the Saint (*Ἔχει μὲν ἡ θειοτάτη σου ψυχή*) and the Troparion of the Earthquake *Ὁ ἐπιβλέπων ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν καὶ ποιῶν αὐτὴν τρέμειν* (Psalm 103:32 LXX) substituted in place of the Theotokion. At Matins (§ 2), two canons are appointed: the Canon of the Earthquake in Tone 6 (by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer, with the penitential refrain *Δόξα σοι, ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, δόξα σοι*) and the second Canon of the Saint in Tone 4 (by Saint Theophanes the Branded, having the acrostic *Μερίς μου, Δημήτριε*); the Troparion of the Earthquake replaces the Theotokion after Ode 3. At the Divine Liturgy (§ 3), the Epistle is 2 Timothy 2:1–10 ("Thou therefore, my son, be strong in the grace that is in Christ Jesus... Endure hardness, as a good soldier of Jesus Christ") and the Gospel is John 15:17–16:2 ("These things I command you, that ye love one another..."). Sections 4–8 provide detailed rubrics for Saturday and Sunday concurrences.

[^167]: Textual Criticism of the Praises Doxastikon of Saint Demetrios and Violakis's Editorial Defense (Violakis, pp. 204–205, October 26, § 7 note 1). This footnote reproduces in full the crucial critical annotation printed at the foot of pages 204–205 in the 1888 edition: "In the Typikon of Constantine [the Byzantios, Protopsaltes of the Great Church], published in 1851, a note appears here correcting the ending of this Doxastikon [at the Praises: *Τὸν λόγχαις κληρωσάμενον* / 'Pierced by spears'], which reads: 'the protector of the Thessalonians in many and frequent dire perils' (*τὸν ἐν πολλοῖς καὶ πολλάκις κινδύνοις χαλεποῖς τῶν Θεσσαλονικέων προϊστάμενον*), altering it to: 'the protector of all the pious in many and dire perils' (*τὸν ἐν πολλοῖς καὶ... χαλεποῖς πᾶσι τοῖς εὐσεβέσι προϊστάμενον*). But because this correction neither succeeds grammatically, nor is it permitted to alter hymns arbitrarily according to one's liking—especially those in which mention is made of events possessing historical significance—for these reasons it is recommended to the chanter that he preserve with all exactitude the text as it is found originally." George Violakis here establishes a primary methodological principle of Constantinopolitan liturgical editorial practice: ancient hymnographic texts recording historical topoi (in this case, Saint Demetrios's specific historical role as miraculous defender and patron of Thessalonica against barbarian sieges) must not be bowdlerized or universalized to satisfy modern stylistic taste.

[^168]: Commemoration of the Holy Unmercenaries Cosmas and Damian of Asia, Sunday Concurrence, and Inter-Cohort Transition to Cohort 23 (Violakis, pp. 204–205, November 1, §§ 1–2 incipit). November 1 begins the liturgical month of November with the memory of the Holy Wonderworkers and Unmercenary Physicians Cosmas and Damian of Asia and their mother Theodote (distinguished from the Roman unmercenaries of July 1 and the Arabian unmercenaries of October 17). On a weekday (§ 1), the service is sung plain according to the Menaion. When occurring on a Sunday (§ 2), Great Vespers on Saturday evening prescribes 6 Resurrection Stichira and 4 Stichira of the Holy Unmercenaries at "Lord, I have cried". Physical leaf p219 (printed page 204) and facing English leaf p220 (printed page 205) terminate syntactically mid-sentence at the Doxastikon clause: *Δόξα τῶν Ἁγίων, Καὶ...* / *Glory of the Saints, [Both now...]* at the foot of the folio. The sentence resumes immediately across the physical leaf and cohort boundary onto printed page 206 (physical leaf p221), which begins Cohort 23 with the First Dogmatic Theotokion of the Tone, the Entrance, the daily Prokeimenon, and the Aposticha.


## Cohort 23 Footnotes

[^169]: Commemoration of the Holy Wonderworkers and Unmercenaries Cosmas and Damian of Asia, Sunday Vespers Propers, and Inter-Cohort Resumption (Violakis, pp. 206–207, November 1, § 2 concl.). Physical leaf p221 (printed page 206) and facing English leaf p222 (printed page 207) begin Cohort 23 with the concluding propers of Saturday Great Vespers for the feast of the Holy Unmercenaries Cosmas and Damian when celebrated on a Sunday, continuing directly across the cohort boundary from printed pages 204–205 (leaves p219–p220, Cohort 22). Following the 6 Resurrection Stichira and 4 Stichira of the Unmercenaries at "Lord, I have cried", the Doxastikon of the Saints is followed at "Both now and ever" by the First Dogmatic Theotokion of the Tone. After the Entrance with the censer and the Saturday evening Prokeimenon (*The Lord is King*, Ps. 92:1 LXX), the Resurrection Aposticha are sung, followed by the Doxastikon of the Saints, the Theotokion *Ὁ ποιητὴς καὶ λυτρωτής μου* ("My Maker and Redeemer"), the Resurrection Dismissal Troparion, the Troparion of the Saints (*Ἅγιοι Ἀνάργυροι καὶ θαυματουργοί*), and the Theotokion *Σὲ τὴν μεσιτεύσασαν* ("Thou who hast mediated for the salvation of our race").

[^170]: Order of Sunday Matins and Divine Liturgy for the Holy Unmercenaries Cosmas and Damian of Asia (Violakis, pp. 206–207, November 1, § 3). When November 1 coincides with a Sunday, the preparatory Matins sequence follows the standard Constantinopolitan parish typikon: after Psalm 50 [LXX], the Triadic Canon is sung followed by the Triadic hymn *Ἄξιόν ἐστιν* ("It is truly meet"), the Trisagion, the Hypakoe of the Tone, and the Hexapsalmos. At the Canons, the Resurrection Canon is combined with that of the Saints; after Ode 3, the Kathisma of the Saints is appointed with its Theotokion; after Ode 6, the Resurrection Kontakion and Ikos are chanted, followed by the Synaxarion from the Menaion. The Katavasiai *Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου* ("I shall open my mouth") are appointed according to the standard post-Byzantine usage prior to the Forefeast of the Entry. At the Divine Liturgy, the Beatitudes assign 4 troparia to the Tone and 4 to Ode 6 of the Canon of the Saints; the Epistle lection is 1 Corinthians 12:27–13:8 (pericope 153, the apostolic charisms of healing and the supremacy of divine love); the Gospel is that of the Sunday; and the Communion Verse is Psalm 148:1 LXX (*Αἰνεῖτε τὸν Κύριον ἐκ τῶν οὐρανῶν* / "Praise the Lord from the heavens").

[^171]: Synaxis of the Archistrategoi Michael and Gabriel and All the Bodiless Powers, Katavasiai of the Entry, and Festal Propers (Violakis, pp. 206–209, November 8, §§ 1–2). The feast of the Synaxis of the Archistrategoi Michael and Gabriel and all the Heavenly Bodiless Powers is ranked among the major non-dominical feasts of the liturgical year. At Great Vespers (§ 1), 6 festal Prosomoia are sung at "Lord, I have cried" followed by three Old Testament lections: Joshua 5:13–15 (the appearance of the Archistrategos of the Lord's host to Joshua before Jericho); Judges 6:11–24 (the Angel of the Lord calling Gideon); and Isaiah 14:7–20 (the fall of the morning star Lucifer). The Aposticha verses are Psalm 103:4 LXX (*Ὁ ποιῶν τοὺς Ἀγγέλους αὐτοῦ πνεύματα* / "Who makes His angels spirits, and His ministers a flame of fire") and Psalm 103:1 LXX. At Matins (§ 2), two canons are sung: the Canon to the Theotokos (*Ὑγρὰν διοδεύσας*, Tone 8) and the second Canon of the Bodiless Powers (*Τοὺς νοεροὺς τῶν Ἀσωμάτων τάξεων*, Tone 4, composed by Saint John the Monk). A distinctive rubrical feature of the Constantinopolitan Typikon is the appointment, beginning on November 8, of the festal Katavasiai of the Entry of the Theotokos (*Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου*) featuring specific Entry-themed incipits: at Ode 1 (*καὶ ᾄσω γηθόμενος ταύτης τὴν Εἴσοδον*), Ode 3 (*Ἐν τῇ σεπτῇ Εἰσόδῳ σου*), Ode 5 (*Ἔνδον εἰσῆλθες ἐν τῷ ναῷ τοῦ Θεοῦ*), and Ode 9 (*τὰ ἱερὰ Εἰσόδια τῆς Θεομήτορος*).

[^172]: Divine Liturgy of the Bodiless Powers and Sunday Concurrence (Violakis, pp. 208–211, November 8, §§ 3–6). For the Divine Liturgy (§ 3), the Beatitudes assign troparia from Odes 3 and 6 of the second Canon of the Archistrategoi. Following the Little Entrance with the Gospel, the festal Introit verse *Ὁ ἐν Ἁγίοις θαυμαστός* ("Who art wonderful in the Saints") is chanted, followed by the Dismissal Troparion *Τῶν Οὐρανίων Στρατιῶν* ("Of the Heavenly Hosts Archistrategoi"), the patronal troparion, and the Kontakion *Ὁ καθαρώτατος Ναὸς τοῦ Σωτῆρος* ("The most pure Temple of the Saviour"). The appointed lections are: Epistle: Hebrews 2:2–10 (pericope 305, Christ made lower than the angels to taste death for every man); Gospel: Luke 10:16–21 (pericope 51, "I beheld Satan as lightning fall from heaven"); and the Communion Verse is Psalm 103:4 LXX. When the feast occurs on a Sunday (§§ 4–6), the Resurrection propers take precedence in the first position at Vespers, Matins, and Divine Liturgy: at Vespers, 4 Resurrection and 6 Archangels; at Matins, the Kathismata interleave the Resurrection and festal texts across three stichologiae; at the Divine Liturgy, the Beatitudes take 4 of the Tone and 4 from Ode 6 of Canon 2, while the Epistle is of the feast and the Gospel is of the Sunday.

[^173]: Commemoration of the Holy Martyrs Menas, Victor, Vincent, and Stephanie; Theodore the Studite; John the Merciful; and Nilus the Ascetic (Violakis, pp. 210–213, November 11, §§ 1–3; November 12, § 1). November 11 commemorates the third-century military martyr Saint Menas of Egypt (d. 296), martyred with Victor and Vincent, and Saint Stephanie; combined with the great monastic reformer and confessor Saint Theodore the Studite (d. 826). Under the Constantinopolitan parish rule, when November 11 falls on a weekday (§ 1), both offices are combined according to the Menaion; however, when falling on a Sunday (§§ 2–3), the office of Saint Theodore is deferred to Compline (*Ἡ δὲ τοῦ Ὁσίου Ἀκολουθία ψάλλεται ἐν τοῖς Ἀποδείπνοις*) to allow the full union of the Resurrection and martyr propers at Vespers and Matins. At the Divine Liturgy (§ 3), the Epistle is 2 Corinthians 4:6–15 (pericope 176, "treasure in earthen vessels") and the Gospel is that of the Sunday. November 12 similarly commemorates Saint John the Merciful, Patriarch of Alexandria (d. 619), and Saint Nilus the Ascetic of Sinai (d. ca. 430); on a Sunday, the office of Saint Nilus is likewise transferred to Compline, and the Divine Liturgy follows the exact structure of November 11 §§ 2–3.

[^174]: Feast of Saint John Chrysostom, Archbishop of Constantinople, Sunday Concurrence, and Inter-Cohort Boundary (Violakis, pp. 212–215, November 13, §§ 1–5). November 13 marks the feast of Saint John Chrysostom (transferred from the historical date of his repose on September 14 due to the Feast of the Universal Exaltation of the Precious Cross). At Great Vespers (§ 1), the First Stasis of the Psalter (*Blessed is the man*, Pss. 1–3 LXX) is sung; at "Lord, I have cried", 3 Prosomoia are doubled to 6 (*Τὴν χρυσήλατον Σάλπιγγα*), followed by three Old Testament lections (Proverbs 10:7, 6; 3:13–16; 8:6 etc.; Proverbs 10:31–11:12; Wisdom 4:7–15); the Dismissal Troparion is the celebrated hierarchal troparion: *Ἡ τοῦ στόματός σου καθάπερ πυρσὸς ἐκλάμψασα χάρις* ("The grace of thy mouth shining forth like a torch hath enlightened the universe"). At Matins (§ 2), the Prokeimenon is Psalm 48:4 LXX (*Τὸ στόμα μου λαλήσει σοφίαν* / "My mouth shall speak wisdom"), the Gospel is John 10:9–16, and the Praises Doxastikon is *Χρυσέοις ἔπεσι*. At the Divine Liturgy (§ 3), the Epistle is Hebrews 7:26–8:2 (pericope 318, "For such an high priest became us") and the Communion Verse is Psalm 111:6 LXX (*Εἰς μνημόσυνον αἰώνιον ἔσται δίκαιος*). When occurring on a Sunday (§§ 4–5), the Resurrection office is combined with that of the Hierarch. Physical leaf p229 (printed page 214) and facing English leaf p230 (printed page 215) conclude Cohort 23 at the Sunday Matins Kathismata clause: *τὸ Ψαλτήριον καὶ ὁ...* / *the Psalter and the Polyeleos. The Resurrection Kathismata in order;...*, transitioning directly to printed page 216 (physical leaf p231) in Cohort 24.


## Cohort 24 Footnotes

[^175]: Commemoration of the Holy Apostle Philip, Sunday Concurrence, and Inter-Cohort Resumption (Violakis, pp. 216–217, November 13 concl. §§ 5–6; November 14, § 1). Physical leaf p231 (printed page 216) and facing English leaf p232 (printed page 217) begin Cohort 24 by concluding the Sunday concurrence for the feast of Saint John Chrysostom, continuing across the cohort boundary from printed pages 214–215 (leaves p229–p230, Cohort 23). Following the Polyeleos at Sunday Matins, the third stichologia assigns the Kathismata of the Saint with their Theotokion; at the Divine Liturgy, the Beatitudes take 4 of the Tone and 4 from Ode 6 of the Hierarch's canon, with the Epistle from 1 Corinthians 4:9–16 (pericope 131) and the Gospel of the Sunday. On November 14, the commemoration of the Holy Apostle Philip on a weekday is governed by the Menaion; when coinciding with a Sunday, the Typikon directs the officiant to follow the rubrical model of the Holy Apostle Luke (October 18), wherein the Apostle's propers are joined with the Resurrectional office: at the Praises, "Glory" is the appointed Sunday Eothinon Doxastikon, and at the Divine Liturgy, the Apostle's lection (1 Corinthians 4:9–16, pericope 131) is chanted before the Sunday Gospel, with the Communion Verse being Psalm 148:1 LXX (*Αἰνεῖτε τὸν Κύριον ἐκ τῶν οὐρανῶν* / "Praise the Lord from the heavens").

[^176]: Commemoration of the Holy Apostle and Evangelist Matthew and Sunday Observance (Violakis, pp. 216–219, November 16, §§ 1–3). On a weekday (§ 1), the service of the Evangelist Matthew follows the Menaion. When November 16 occurs on a Sunday (§§ 2–3), Saturday Great Vespers appoints 6 Resurrection Stichira and 4 of the Evangelist at "Lord, I have cried", followed at "Glory" by the Doxastikon of the Apostle, and at "Both now and ever" by the First Dogmatic Theotokion of the Tone. At the Aposticha, the Resurrection Stichira are followed by "Glory" of the Evangelist, "Both now and ever" (*Ὁ ποιητὴς καὶ λυτρωτής μου* / "My Maker and Redeemer"), the Resurrection Dismissal Troparion, the Troparion of the Evangelist (*Μαθητὴς χρηματίσας*), and the Theotokion *Σὲ τὴν μεσιτεύσασαν* ("Thou who hast mediated for the salvation of our race"). At Sunday Matins (§ 3), after the Third Ode, the Kontakion of the Evangelist (*Τοῦ κηρύγματος τὴν σάλπιγγα δεδεγμένος*) is chanted followed by his Kathisma; after the Sixth Ode, the Resurrection Kontakion and Ikos are chanted, followed by the Synaxarion from the Menaion. At the Divine Liturgy, the Beatitudes assign 4 to the Tone and 4 to Ode 6 of the Evangelist's canon; the appointed lections are: Epistle: 1 Corinthians 4:9–16 (pericope 131, apostolic sufferings for Christ); Gospel: Matthew 9:9–13 (pericope 30, the calling of Matthew at the receipt of custom); and the Communion Verse is Psalm 18:5 LXX (*Εἰς πᾶσαν τὴν γῆν ἐξῆλθεν ὁ φθόγγος αὐτῶν* / "Their sound hath gone forth into all the earth, and their words unto the ends of the world").

[^177]: Forefeast of the Entrance of the Most Holy Theotokos, Saints Gregory the Decapolite and Proclus, and Violakis's Critical Erratum on the Forefeast Kathisma (Violakis, pp. 218–221, November 20, §§ 1–4). November 20 commemorates the Forefeast of the Entrance of the Mother of God, joined with Saint Gregory the Decapolite (d. 842) and Saint Proclus, Archbishop of Constantinople and disciple of Chrysostom (d. 446; printed as "Patroclos" in the 1888 edition). When November 20 coincides with a Sunday (§§ 2–4), the Saturday Great Vespers assigns 6 Resurrection Stichira and 4 of the Forefeast at "Lord, I have cried"; at the Aposticha, the Doxastikon at "Glory; Both now and ever" is *Δεῦτε πάντες οἱ πιστοί* ("Come, all ye faithful"); and the Dismissal Troparion of the Forefeast is *Χαρὰν προμνηστεύεται σήμερον ἡ Ἄννα* ("Anna betrotheth unto us joy today"). At Sunday Matins (§ 3), the Forefeast Kathismata replace the usual Theotokia after each stichologia of the Psalter. Crucially, at Ode 3 of the canons, the printed 1888 text contains a famous rubrical erratum flagged explicitly by the editor in brackets: the rubric appointed *Εὐφροσύνης σήμερον* ("Filled with joy today") as the Ode 3 Kathisma, but Violakis notes (*Αὐτὸ πρέπει νὰ εἶναι λάθος...*) that *Εὐφροσύνης σήμερον* is actually the Forefeast Kontakion (sung at the Divine Liturgy, § 4), whereas the Kathisma appointed in the Menaion is *Ὁ ναὸς τοῦ Θεοῦ* ("The temple of God"). At the Divine Liturgy (§ 4), the Beatitudes assign 4 of the Tone and 4 from Ode 6 of the Forefeast canon; the lections are of the Sunday; and the Communion Verse is Psalm 148:1 LXX.

[^178]: The Great Feast of the Entrance of the Most Holy Theotokos into the Temple: Vespers, Matins, and Ninth Ode Festal Megalynaria (Violakis, pp. 220–223, November 21, §§ 5–6). November 21 marks the Entry of the Most Holy Theotokos into the Temple, ranked among the Twelve Great Feasts of the liturgical year. At Great Vespers (§ 5), following the First Stasis of the Psalter (*Blessed is the man*, Pss. 1–3 LXX), 6 festal Stichira are sung at "Lord, I have cried" (*Σήμερον πιστοί* and *Τῶν Ἁγίων εἰς Ἅγια*), followed by three Old Testament composite lections: Exodus 40:1–5, 9–10, 16, 34–35 (the erection and consecration of the Tabernacle of Witness and the cloud of the glory of the Lord); 1 Kings 8:1, 3–7, 9–11 (the translation of the Ark of the Covenant into the Holy of Holies of Solomon's Temple); and Ezekiel 43:27–44:4 (the vision of the closed East Gate through which none but the Lord God of Israel enters). At the Aposticha, the Dismissal Troparion *Σήμερον τῆς εὐδοκίας θεοῦ τὸ προοίμιον* ("Today is the prelude of the goodwill of God") is chanted thrice. At Matins (§ 6), the Polyeleos is the Marian Psalm 44 LXX (*Ἐξηρεύξατο ἡ καρδία μου* / "My heart hath poured forth a good word"), the Prokeimenon is Psalm 44:11 LXX (*Ἄκουσον, θύγατερ, καὶ ἴδε* / "Hear, O daughter, and see"), and the Gospel is Luke 1:39–49, 56 (the Visitation to Elizabeth and the Magnificat). At Ode 9, the two festal canons (First Canon in Tone 4 by George of Nicomedia, Second Canon in Tone 1 by Basil of Paganiote) are sung with their distinctive Ninth Ode Megalynaria: the Tone 4 refrain *Ἄγγελοι τὴν Εἴσοδον τῆς Παρθένου ὁρῶντες ἐξεπλήττοντο* ("The Angels, beholding the Entrance of the Virgin, were struck with wonder") and the Tone 1 refrain *Μεγάλυνον ψυχή μου τὴν προσενεχθεῖσαν ἐν τῷ Ναῷ Κυρίου* ("Magnify, O my soul, her who was brought into the Temple of the Lord"). The Katavasia of Ode 9 is *Μυστήριον ξένον* ("A strange and wonderful mystery"), and the Dismissal Troparion is chanted at the end of the Great Doxology.

[^179]: Festal Antiphons of the Divine Liturgy for the Entrance of the Theotokos and Inter-Cohort Boundary (Violakis, pp. 224–225, November 21, § 7). At the Divine Liturgy for the Entrance of the Mother of God, the daily Typika and Beatitudes are replaced by the three proper Festal Antiphons drawn from the Septuagint Psalter: Antiphon 1 is drawn from Psalm 47:2, 4, 9 LXX and Psalm 86:3 LXX (*Μέγας Κύριος καὶ αἰνετὸς σφόδρα*, *Δεδοξασμένα ἐλαλήθη περὶ σοῦ*, *Ὁ Θεὸς ἐν ταῖς βάρεσιν αὐτῆς*, *Καθάπερ ἠκούσαμεν οὕτω καὶ εἴδομεν*), with the refrain *Ταῖς πρεσβείαις τῆς Θεοτόκου*; Antiphon 2 is drawn from Psalm 45:5 LXX, Psalm 95:6 LXX, Psalm 117:20 LXX, and Psalm 64:5–6 LXX (*Ἠγίασε τὸ σκήνωμα αὐτοῦ ὁ Ὕψιστος*, *Ἁγιωσύνη καὶ μεγαλοπρέπεια*, *Αὕτη ἡ πύλη Κυρίου*, *Ἅγιος ὁ ναός σου*), followed by the hymnic doxology *Ὁ Μονογενὴς Υἱὸς καὶ Λόγος τοῦ Θεοῦ*; and Antiphon 3 is drawn from Psalm 44:13, 14, 15 LXX (*Τὸ πρόσωπόν σου λιτανεύσουσιν*, *Πᾶσα ἡ Δόξα τῆς θυγατρὸς τοῦ βασιλέως*, *Ἀπενεχθήσονται τῷ Βασιλεῖ παρθένοι ὀπίσω αὐτῆς*), with the refrain being the festal Dismissal Troparion *Σήμερον τῆς εὐδοκίας θεοῦ τὸ προοίμιον*. Physical leaf p239 (printed page 224) and facing English leaf p240 (printed page 225) conclude Cohort 24 at the final verse of Antiphon 3, transitioning immediately across the cohort boundary to physical leaf p241 (printed page 226) in Cohort 25 for the Little Entrance with the Gospel, the Introit verse, the Apolytikia, the Kontakion, and the festal Eucharistic lections.


## Cohort 25 Footnotes

[^180]: Concurrence of the Feast of the Entrance of the Most Holy Theotokos into the Temple with a Sunday (Violakis, pp. 226–227, November 21, §§ 8–10). Physical leaf p241 (printed page 226) and facing English leaf p242 (printed page 227) begin Cohort 25 by concluding § 7 of the feast (the Little Entrance, Eucharistic lections, Megalynarion, and Communion Verse) from Cohort 24, and providing the full rubrical order for when November 21 coincides with a Sunday (§§ 8–10). At Saturday Great Vespers (§ 8), 4 Resurrection Stichira and 6 of the feast are sung at "Lord, I have cried", followed at "Glory; Both now and ever" by the festal Doxastikon, the Entrance with the censer, and the three Old Testament readings (Exodus 40:1–5, 9–10, 16, 34–35; 1 Kings 8:1, 3–7, 9–11; Ezekiel 43:27–44:4). At Sunday Matins (§ 9), following the Polyeleos, the Kathismata are distributed across three stichologia: at the First Stichologia, 2 Resurrection Kathismata and the festal Kathisma *Δικαίων ὁ καρπός* ("The fruit of the righteous"); at the Second Stichologia, 2 Resurrection Kathismata and *Δαβὶδ προοδοποίησον* ("David, prepare the way"); and at the Third Stichologia, two festal Kathismata (*Πρὸ συλλήψεως ἁγνή* and *Δαβὶδ ὁ προπάτωρ*). The Matins Gospel is Luke 1:39–49, 56 (*Ἀναστᾶσα Μαριάμ*), followed by *Ἀνάστασιν Χριστοῦ θεασάμενοι* and Psalm 50 LXX. At the canons, the Resurrection Canon is joined with the two festal canons; after Ode 3, the Resurrection Kontakion and Ikos are recited, followed by the festal Kathisma (Violakis notes an editorial erratum in the printed Greek text mentioning "two Kathismata", whereas the Menaion appoints a single Kathisma chanted twice); after Ode 6, the festal Kontakion and Ikos are sung with the Synaxarion; and at Ode 9, the festal Megalynaria are chanted with the Katavasia *Μυστήριον ξένον* ("A strange and wonderful mystery"). At the Praises, 4 Resurrection Stichira and 4 of the feast are sung, with the festal Doxastikon *Σήμερον τῷ Ναῷ προσάγεται* ("Today into the Temple is brought") at "Glory".

[^181]: Sunday after the Entrance of the Most Holy Theotokos into the Temple (Violakis, pp. 228–229, November 21–24 Afterfeast, §§ 11–13). When a Sunday falls during the Afterfeast of the Entrance (November 22–24), the rubrics govern the union of the Sunday Resurrectional office with the Afterfeast propers. At Saturday Great Vespers (§ 11), 6 Resurrection Stichira are combined with 4 Afterfeast Stichira from the Menaion series at "Lord, I have cried"; at "Glory", the festal Doxastikon is chanted, and at "Both now and ever", the First Dogmatic Theotokion of the Tone. At Sunday Matins (§ 12), the Psalter stichologia assigns the Resurrection Kathismata, with the Afterfeast Kathismata substituting for the usual Theotokia; the canons combine the Resurrection Canon with the Second Canon of the feast; after Ode 3, the Afterfeast Kathisma is chanted; after Ode 6, the Resurrection Kontakion and Ikos are sung with the Menaion Synaxarion; and the Katavasiai of the Nativity (*Χριστὸς γεννᾶται* / "Christ is born, give ye glory") are chanted, followed by the normal Sunday Morning Gospel sequence. At the Praises, 4 Resurrection Stichira and 4 Afterfeast Stichira are sung, followed at "Glory" by the Sunday Eothinon Doxastikon. At the Divine Liturgy (§ 13), the Beatitudes assign 4 to the Tone and 4 to Ode 6 of the festal canon; the Epistle and Gospel are of the Sunday; and the Communion Verse is Psalm 148:1 LXX (*Αἰνεῖτε τὸν Κύριον ἐκ τῶν οὐρανῶν* / "Praise the Lord from the heavens").

[^182]: Apodosis of the Entrance of the Most Holy Theotokos, Commemoration of the Great-Martyrs Catherine and Mercurius, Sunday Concurrence, and the Nativity Pre-festal Kontakion Rubric (Violakis, pp. 228–233, November 25, §§ 14–19). November 25 celebrates the Apodosis (concluding leave-taking) of the Entrance of the Theotokos, joined with the commemoration of the Holy Great-Martyr Catherine of Alexandria and the Holy Great-Martyr Mercurius. On a weekday (§§ 14–16), Great Vespers appoints 3 Prosomoia of the feast (*Σήμερον πιστοὶ χορεύσωμεν*) and 3 of Saint Catherine at "Lord, I have cried"; at Matins, both canons of the feast and the canon of the Saint are chanted, with the Ninth Ode sung with the festal Megalynaria; and at the Divine Liturgy, the proper Festal Antiphons are sung, with the Epistle (Galatians 3:23–4:5) and Gospel (Mark 5:24–34) of Saint Catherine. When November 25 coincides with a Sunday (§§ 17–19), Saturday Great Vespers assigns 4 Resurrection Stichira, 3 of the feast, and 3 of the Saint; at Sunday Matins, the stichologia Kathismata combine the Resurrection Kathismata with those of the feast (substituting for the Theotokia), and at the Praises, 4 Resurrection Stichira are joined with 4 of the Saint, with the festal Doxastikon *Σήμερον τῷ Ναῷ προσάγεται* at "Glory". At the Divine Liturgy (§ 19), the Festal Antiphons are sung with the Sunday refrain *Ὁ ἀναστὰς ἐκ νεκρῶν* at the Second Antiphon, and the Epistle of Saint Catherine is joined with the Sunday Gospel. Most significantly, an overarching rubrical note at the conclusion of § 19 establishes that following the Apodosis of the Entrance, on the very next Sunday or feast day, the Church commences chanting the Forefeast Kontakion of the Nativity of Christ: *Ἡ παρθένος σήμερον τὸν προαιώνιον Λόγον* ("Today the Virgin cometh to the cave to give birth ineffably to the Pre-eternal Word").

[^183]: Feast of the Holy Apostle Andrew the First-Called: Weekday Typikon (Violakis, pp. 232–235, November 30, §§ 1–3). The feast of Saint Andrew the First-Called is celebrated with full festal solemnity on November 30. At Great Vespers on a weekday (§ 1), following the First Stasis of the Psalter (*Blessed is the man*, Pss. 1–3 LXX), 3 Prosomoia are doubled to 6 at "Lord, I have cried" (*Ὁ τῷ Προδρόμου φωτὶ μεμορφωμένος*), followed at "Glory" by *Τὴν τῶν ἰχθύων ἄγραν* and at "Both now and ever" by the Nativity Forefeast Theotokion *Ἡσαΐα χόρευε* ("Rejoice, O Isaiah"); three Old Testament composite readings drawn from the Catholic Epistles of Saint Peter (1 Peter 1:1–2:6; 2:11–24; 3:8–4:11) are read; and the Aposticha conclude with "Glory" (*Τὸν συναίμονα Πέτρου* / "The kinsman of Peter") and the Forefeast Theotokion *Ἰωσὴφ εἰπὲ ἡμῖν* ("Joseph, tell us"). At Matins (§ 2), after the Polyeleos and the First Antiphon of Tone 4, the Prokeimenon is Psalm 18:5 LXX (*Εἰς πᾶσαν τὴν γῆν ἐξῆλθεν ὁ φθόγγος αὐτῶν* / "Their sound hath gone forth into all the earth"), and the Gospel is Matthew 4:18–23 (the calling of Peter and Andrew by the Sea of Galilee). Three canons are chanted: the Canon of the Theotokos, the Menaion Canon, and the Apostle's Canon composed by Saint John Damascene (*Τὴν τεθολωμένην μου ψυχήν*); the Katavasiai are *Χριστὸς γεννᾶται*; and at the Praises, 4 Prosomoia (*Βηθσαϊδὰ νῦν ἀγάλλου*) are sung, concluding at "Glory" with *Τὸν κήρυκα τῆς πίστεως* and at "Both now and ever" with the Forefeast Theotokion *Ὑπόδεξαι Βηθλεέμ* ("Receive, O Bethlehem"). At the Divine Liturgy (§ 3), the Beatitudes take 4 from Ode 3 of the first canon and 4 from Ode 6 of the second canon; the lections are Epistle: 1 Corinthians 4:9–16 (pericope 131) and Gospel: John 1:35–51 (pericope 4, the First-Called Apostle following the Lamb of God); and the Communion Verse is Psalm 18:5 LXX.

[^184]: Feast of the Holy Apostle Andrew the First-Called: Sunday Concurrence (Violakis, pp. 234–235, November 30, §§ 4–5). When the feast of Saint Andrew coincides with a Sunday, the Saturday Great Vespers (§ 4) appoints 6 Resurrection Stichira and 4 of the Apostle at "Lord, I have cried", followed at "Glory" by the Doxastikon of the Apostle and at "Both now and ever" by the First Dogmatic Theotokion of the Tone. At the Aposticha, the Resurrection Stichira are followed by "Glory" (*Τὸν συναίμονα Πέτρου*) and "Both now and ever" (*Ἰωσὴφ εἰπὲ ἡμῖν*). At Sunday Matins (§ 5), the stichologia Kathismata alternate: one Resurrection Kathisma and one of the Apostle with the Theotokion; at the Third Stichologia, the Kathisma of the Apostle is sung with its Theotokion. The canons combine the Resurrection Canon with the First Canon of the Apostle (*Τὴν τεθολωμένην μου ψυχήν*); after Ode 3, the Resurrection Kontakion and Ikos are recited plain, followed by the Kathisma of the Apostle and the Theotokion; after Ode 6, the Kontakion and Ikos of the Apostle are sung with the Menaion Synaxarion; and the Katavasiai are *Χριστὸς γεννᾶται*. At the Praises, 4 Resurrection Stichira and 4 of the Apostle are sung; at "Glory", the Doxastikon of the Apostle *Τὸν κήρυκα τῆς πίστεως* is sung in place of the Sunday Eothinon Doxastikon, followed at "Both now and ever" by *Ὑπερευλογημένη*. At the Sunday Divine Liturgy, the Typika and Beatitudes combine the Tone and the Apostle's canon, joining the Apostolic lections with the Resurrectional office.
